← Back to the case report · CORD Reports
Extended by a later full static re-analysis. CORD's pass below could not decode the download URL or read the ten RCDATA resources. The re-analysis did: the XOR-encoded URL is hxxps://koooolda[.]shop/43413, and resource 103 is the relay kit's client script, which POSTs DyNaM1c-prefixed actions (visit, doLogin, submitCode, checkConfirmations) XOR-encrypted with the affiliate cookie's secret. The program serves a local Steam sign-in UI over WebView2 to that kit.

Static analysis · PE · 2026-10-29

Unattributed malicious

MaliciousRole: loaderFamily: not attributed

An 849 KB x64 PE built with Visual Studio 2019 (E15) that masquerades as a Steam client component (E16). It finds the Steam install directory through registry queries (B2, E2, E3), copies itself there (B5, E6), writes registry persistence (B3, E4) and hijacks shortcuts in six shell folders through IShellLink (B12, E14, E18). A Steam-looking lure window fires a timer that downloads a secondary payload (B1, B7, E1, E17). The installer first stops a service (B11, E13) and closes processes by name (B4, E5). Runtime strings are XOR-encoded (B9, E11). The operator gets a loader that replaces a legitimate application, persists, and fetches further stages while the victim keeps using Steam.

How it runs

The behaviours as a flow, from the entry point: the checks that can end the run, then what it collects, then what it sends and what follows. Boxes follow the order of the analyst's account; a dashed box is code whose path from the entry is not proven. Static analysis: not an observed run.

Throughout: xor_string_decode (B9).

What it does in the full report →

The CRT stub passes the module handle to WinMain (B7, E8). WinMain resolves CopyFileW and MoveFileW dynamically from kernel32, then calls the installer at 0x140007950 (B5, E6).

The installer checks for elevation through OpenProcessToken and GetTokenInformation (B10, E12). If not elevated it relaunches itself with the runas verb and exits. Elevated, it opens a service through OpenSCManager and sends a stop control (B11, E13), then walks a process snapshot, compares names and posts WM_CLOSE to matching windows (B4, E5). This clears the target application before it is overwritten.

The file-replace function at 0x140007680 deletes the original Steam executable and copies the sample over it with the dynamically resolved CopyFileW and MoveFileW (B8, E10). The persistence function at 0x140006d70 writes shell-open-command and uninstall keys under HKCU and HKLM, with XOR-decoded key names and a random sleep (B3, E4). A helper at 0x140005f00 reads the Steam path from HKLM SOFTWARE WOW6432Node and per-user uninstall keys (B2, E2, E3; I1).

The installer then searches six shell folders (Desktop, Start Menu, AppData and their common counterparts) recursively to depth 9, matching with PathMatchSpecW (B6, E7). The hijacker at 0x140007270 loads each .lnk through IShellLink, compares the target filename with wcsicoll and rewrites the target path, arguments and icon to the installed copy (B12, E14, E18). A helper at 0x140007150 normalizes the link extension (E20). Launching the copy from any shortcut gives the operator execution each time the victim starts Steam.

On success WinMain extracts the Steam icon with ExtractIconW, registers a window class and creates a 700x500 layered window (B7, E8). The lure parses URL navigation events and launches them with ShellExecuteW (E9). Its WndProc, at 0x140005ae0, sets timer 1; the WM_TIMER handler kills the timer and calls the download function at 0x140005750, which fetches a file to the temp directory with URLDownloadToFileW from an XOR-decoded URL (B1, E1, E17). WM_SIZE applies a 12 px rounded region (E17, E19).

Two decode mechanisms protect strings: a 0x5A5A word XOR for registry paths and a DWORD key at 0x14002a510 applied to a 560-byte block at 0x14002a2e0 (B9, E11).

Not recovered: the decoded download URL, file names, service name, process names, registry paths and shortcut wildcard, because the decode was only read statically (U1); the ten RCDATA resources (IDs 103-113, about 650 KB), which are data and text of entropy 4.67-6.07 (U4).

How much of the code was read functions →

The native call graph found 632 internal functions and reached 357 from entry. Dispatch resolution raised that to 390 (62%); the Ghidra graph agrees with the native one at 357. Of the 242 unreached functions, CRT and library code were filtered by Ghidra FID and capa library rules; 25 unresolved call sites remain in reached code, so the unreached set stays possibly reached.

Verdict and detection opportunities all anchor details →

Malicious loader (E2, E6, E8, E11, E14, E17, E18), confidence 0.4. No family is assigned: nothing known matches the combination of a Steam-path lure window, shortcut hijack and XOR-decoded download, which agrees with the cluster label pe/unk.steamlure-28f2b618.

Strongest anchors: A3, the installer function, whose structure holds the elevation, service stop, process kill, file replacement and persistence logic (E6). A1, the Steam registry query sequence with XOR-decoded subkey in the function at 0x140006170, is the targeting mechanism and would need a rewrite to change (E2, E3). A2, the DWORD-block XOR with 0x5A5A tail, is distinctive and build-specific (E11). A4 (rich header, E15) and A5 (version info, E16) are metadata filters that change on recompile or edit.

Summary

An 849 KB x64 PE built with Visual Studio 2019 (E15) that masquerades as a Steam client component (E16). It finds the Steam install directory through registry queries (B2, E2, E3), copies itself there (B5, E6), writes registry persistence (B3, E4) and hijacks shortcuts in six shell folders through IShellLink (B12, E14, E18). A Steam-looking lure window fires a timer that downloads a secondary payload (B1, B7, E1, E17). The installer first stops a service (B11, E13) and closes processes by name (B4, E5). Runtime strings are XOR-encoded (B9, E11). The operator gets a loader that replaces a legitimate application, persists, and fetches further stages while the victim keeps using Steam.

Behaviour

The CRT stub passes the module handle to WinMain (B7, E8). WinMain resolves CopyFileW and MoveFileW dynamically from kernel32, then calls the installer at 0x140007950 (B5, E6).

The installer checks for elevation through OpenProcessToken and GetTokenInformation (B10, E12). If not elevated it relaunches itself with the runas verb and exits. Elevated, it opens a service through OpenSCManager and sends a stop control (B11, E13), then walks a process snapshot, compares names and posts WM_CLOSE to matching windows (B4, E5). This clears the target application before it is overwritten.

The file-replace function at 0x140007680 deletes the original Steam executable and copies the sample over it with the dynamically resolved CopyFileW and MoveFileW (B8, E10). The persistence function at 0x140006d70 writes shell-open-command and uninstall keys under HKCU and HKLM, with XOR-decoded key names and a random sleep (B3, E4). A helper at 0x140005f00 reads the Steam path from HKLM SOFTWARE WOW6432Node and per-user uninstall keys (B2, E2, E3; I1).

The installer then searches six shell folders (Desktop, Start Menu, AppData and their common counterparts) recursively to depth 9, matching with PathMatchSpecW (B6, E7). The hijacker at 0x140007270 loads each .lnk through IShellLink, compares the target filename with wcsicoll and rewrites the target path, arguments and icon to the installed copy (B12, E14, E18). A helper at 0x140007150 normalizes the link extension (E20). Launching the copy from any shortcut gives the operator execution each time the victim starts Steam.

On success WinMain extracts the Steam icon with ExtractIconW, registers a window class and creates a 700x500 layered window (B7, E8). The lure parses URL navigation events and launches them with ShellExecuteW (E9). Its WndProc, at 0x140005ae0, sets timer 1; the WM_TIMER handler kills the timer and calls the download function at 0x140005750, which fetches a file to the temp directory with URLDownloadToFileW from an XOR-decoded URL (B1, E1, E17). WM_SIZE applies a 12 px rounded region (E17, E19).

Two decode mechanisms protect strings: a 0x5A5A word XOR for registry paths and a DWORD key at 0x14002a510 applied to a 560-byte block at 0x14002a2e0 (B9, E11).

Not recovered: the decoded download URL, file names, service name, process names, registry paths and shortcut wildcard, because the decode was only read statically (U1); the ten RCDATA resources (IDs 103-113, about 650 KB), which are data and text of entropy 4.67-6.07 (U4).

Reach

The native call graph found 632 internal functions and reached 357 from entry. Dispatch resolution raised that to 390 (62%); the Ghidra graph agrees with the native one at 357. Of the 242 unreached functions, CRT and library code were filtered by Ghidra FID and capa library rules; 25 unresolved call sites remain in reached code, so the unreached set stays possibly reached.

Verdict and anchors

Malicious loader (E2, E6, E8, E11, E14, E17, E18), confidence 0.4. No family is assigned: nothing known matches the combination of a Steam-path lure window, shortcut hijack and XOR-decoded download, which agrees with the cluster label pe/unk.steamlure-28f2b618.

Strongest anchors: A3, the installer function, whose structure holds the elevation, service stop, process kill, file replacement and persistence logic (E6). A1, the Steam registry query sequence with XOR-decoded subkey in the function at 0x140006170, is the targeting mechanism and would need a rewrite to change (E2, E3). A2, the DWORD-block XOR with 0x5A5A tail, is distinctive and build-specific (E11). A4 (rich header, E15) and A5 (version info, E16) are metadata filters that change on recompile or edit.

Terms used in this report
Original file, unpacked file
The original file is the sample as collected. An unpacked file is one the analysis extracted or decoded from it, numbered in order (in report.json: L0, L1, L2…).
Reachable from the entry point
The static call graph shows a path from where the program starts to this code. It does not mean the code was seen running.
Code present, path not proven
The code is in the file, but the analysis could not prove a route to it, usually because of indirect calls or jump tables.
B, E, A, U, D numbers
Behaviours, evidence items, detection anchors, open questions (unknowns) and derived files (such as decoded strings). Every claim cites the evidence items it rests on.
API hashing
Looking up Windows functions at run time by a hash of their name, so the names never appear in the file.
MBA
Mixed boolean-arithmetic: computing a simple constant through a tangle of AND, OR, XOR and arithmetic.
ATT&CK, MBC
Public catalogues of attacker techniques (MITRE ATT&CK) and malware behaviours (MBC).
IOC
Indicator of compromise: a value, such as a domain or file name, that shows up on an infected host or network.
